The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_controlled}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 16 relations, the extreme-batch gap is 9.1\\,pp; at 80 relations it reaches 31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable at count 16 (CI: [22.9, 39.6]). An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p = 1.5 \\times 10^{-5}$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\nThe larger absolute gap at 80 relations does not reflect stronger per-unit sensitivity; the within-pool analysis below shows slopes are indistinguishable across pools ($p = 0.60$). The 80-relation pool simply spans a wider BCS range, enabling more extreme batches.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nA potential confound in the cross-scale comparison is that the 16-relation and 80-relation pools contain different types, so the observed gap could reflect pool composition rather than confusability. To control for this, we draw 100 random 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool and evaluate under the same BCS-controlled protocol (Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{4}{c}{\\textbf{Regression (BCS $\\to$ Accuracy)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Quartile $\\Delta$}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nIndependent 16-rel ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}10^{-6}$ & $-$5.31 & [$-$7.44, $-$3.19] \\\\\n80-pool $\\to$ 16-sub ($n{=}100$) & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 & $-$3.92 & [$-$8.17, $+$0.38] \\\\\nFull 80-rel ($n{=}25$)$^\\dagger$ & $+$0.221 & [$-$1.303, $+$1.695] & $+$0.064 & 0.762 & $-$1.04 & [$-$10.12, $+$8.04] \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Permutation test (16-rel vs.\\ 80-pool slopes): $p = 0.598$ (5{,}000 permutations)} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^\\dagger$BCS range (IQR\\,=\\,0.033) insufficient for regression; slope not interpretable.} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}\n\\label{tab:within_pool}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe within-pool slope ($-$0.401, CI excludes zero) is statistically indistinguishable from the independent 16-relation pool ($-$0.509; permutation $p = 0.598$). The smaller quartile gap ($-$3.92\\,pp vs.\\ $-$5.31\\,pp) reflects the narrower BCS range within the 80-relation pool (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool}). This confirms the 80-relation extreme-batch gap (31.2\\,pp) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\nIn the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p=0.762$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect---the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied---but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the 31.2\\,pp extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $r^2{=}1.5\\%$ aggregates to batch-level $R^2{=}22$--$53\\%$ across datasets, a 14--35$\\times$ amplification consistent with averaging over $\\sim\\binom{k}{2}$ correlated pair effects per batch.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt (stricter format constraints), the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.0001$). Compared to v1's $-$31.2\\,pp, the effect attenuates modestly ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant. Part of this attenuation reflects the narrower BCS range available under our v2 balanced sampling (0.36--0.80 vs.\\ 0.47--0.97); however, the stronger per-pair correlation ($r = -0.919$) under v2 suggests that per-unit sensitivity to confusability is at least as large when frequency and sample-size confounds are controlled. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates the magnitude but does not eliminate the confusability effect (full analysis in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 10^{-6}$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count $\\to$ recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability $\\to$ type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact.\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 10^{-10}$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

1	\section{Schema Decomposition: Failure, Diagnosis, and Mitigation}
2	\label{sec:sdgd}
3	
4	A natural intervention is to partition the schema so confusable types never co-occur. We test this with \textbf{Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition} (SDGD), which uses greedy graph coloring on the pairwise SCS graph to assign types to low-confusability batches, merging predictions via set union. Every decomposition mode---SDGD (F1\,=\,0.241), random batching (0.320), per-type (0.292)---falls below the single-pass baseline (0.445) on Few-NERD NER (Table~\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).
5	
6	\paragraph{Merge amplification as the bottleneck.}
7	The failure is \emph{merge amplification}: presenting the same text $k$ times causes each pass to independently generate predictions. Single-pass precision (0.563) collapses to 0.210--0.249 after union merge with 8 batches, while recall gains are modest (0.368 $\to$ 0.314--0.446). Confidence gating improves precision but with disproportionate recall loss (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_sdgd}). The fundamental issue is that LLM predictions are not calibrated across passes \cite{llm-calibration-survey-2024}.
8	
9	In our NER experiments, multi-pass decomposition produces $k$ independent tagging outputs per sentence. Entities may receive conflicting labels across separate decomposition passes, or hallucinated entities may appear in passes where the correct type is absent. Union merge amplifies these false positives: precision collapses from 0.563 (single-pass) to 0.210--0.249 (8 batches), faster than the modest recall gains.
10	
11	\paragraph{Why NER fails but RE does not.}
12	Merge amplification is specific to span-level tasks: each NER pass independently proposes entity spans, and union merge must resolve conflicting boundaries and type assignments, degrading systematically with pass count. Under oracle routing, RE requires no span reconciliation---each pair receives a single classification from the batch containing its gold type, eliminating the mechanism that caused SDGD to fail.
13	
14	\paragraph{Confusability-aware batch composition for RE.}
15	Since RE avoids merge amplification, we test whether confusability-aware batching improves accuracy. All decomposition conditions use oracle routing (each instance is routed to the batch containing its gold type). Single-type batches versus the 80-relation single-pass yields +29.8\,pp (0.802 vs.\ 0.504), confirming that decomposition is effective when span reconciliation is unnecessary. Confusability-aware graph coloring achieves 0.802 accuracy versus random batching (0.788 $\pm$ 0.018), a +1.4\,pp gain that is directionally consistent but not statistically significant ($p \approx 0.19$), limited by the single graph-coloring configuration ($n{=}1$) and narrow BCS margin (0.006) achievable at this partition granularity (Table~\ref{tab:batch_composition} in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_batch_composition}).
16	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}
5	The two-channel model applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas (e.g., MAVEN's FrameNet-derived event types) exhibit centrality confounding that obscures per-type aggregated signals, requiring pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\approx$51\% across tested models, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty ceiling. RE's clean channel separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\,pp vs.\ v1: $-$31.2\,pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Practitioner guidance.}
8	Our results suggest that type pairs with high SCS tend to produce disproportionately more substitution errors (\S4.3). Practitioners designing extraction schemas may benefit from pre-computing pairwise SCS and restructuring or merging types that exceed this threshold.
9	
10	\paragraph{Future directions.}
11	Three directions merit investigation: (i)~non-oracle routing strategies (e.g., embedding-based instance-to-batch assignment) that would make decomposition deployable without gold labels; (ii)~broader model coverage, particularly proprietary APIs; and (iii)~definition-inclusive SCS that incorporates type descriptions rather than names alone. For NER, merge amplification blocks naive decomposition (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}); calibration-aware merge, joint decoding, or coarse-to-fine extraction may address this.
12	
13	\section{Conclusion}
14	\label{sec:conclusion}
15	
16	We isolated schema-induced degradation into count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type confusion, showed these channels are separable in RE, with Channel~2 additionally impairing entity detection in NER, and confirmed that both channels replicate across model families on RE (Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: count $r = 0.725$, confusability $\Delta = -27$\,pp) and across extraction granularities (DocRED document-level RE: count decline $-$49\,pp; BCS slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), with high-BCS accuracy converging to a floor consistent across both tested models. We further identified a centrality confound on same-domain schemas, demonstrated that merge amplification is a structural challenge for NER decomposition, and showed that confusability-aware grouping yields a directionally consistent but non-significant improvement (+1.4\,pp, $p\approx 0.19$). Cross-batch calibration or joint decoding for NER, and broader validation of RE batch composition, are the key next steps.
17

  "uuid": "85353fd0-dfab-4e0d-966e-94d5cff1ddae",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "type": "text",
    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}\nThe two-channel model applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas (e.g., MAVEN's FrameNet-derived event types) exhibit centrality confounding that obscures per-type aggregated signals, requiring pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% across tested models, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty ceiling. RE's clean channel separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\\,pp vs.\\ v1: $-$31.2\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practitioner guidance.}\nOur results suggest that type pairs with high SCS tend to produce disproportionately more substitution errors (\\S4.3). Practitioners designing extraction schemas may benefit from pre-computing pairwise SCS and restructuring or merging types that exceed this threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nThree directions merit investigation: (i)~non-oracle routing strategies (e.g., embedding-based instance-to-batch assignment) that would make decomposition deployable without gold labels; (ii)~broader model coverage, particularly proprietary APIs; and (iii)~definition-inclusive SCS that incorporates type descriptions rather than names alone. For NER, merge amplification blocks naive decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}); calibration-aware merge, joint decoding, or coarse-to-fine extraction may address this.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe isolated schema-induced degradation into count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type confusion, showed these channels are separable in RE, with Channel~2 additionally impairing entity detection in NER, and confirmed that both channels replicate across model families on RE (Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: count $r = 0.725$, confusability $\\Delta = -27$\\,pp) and across extraction granularities (DocRED document-level RE: count decline $-$49\\,pp; BCS slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), with high-BCS accuracy converging to a floor consistent across both tested models. We further identified a centrality confound on same-domain schemas, demonstrated that merge amplification is a structural challenge for NER decomposition, and showed that confusability-aware grouping yields a directionally consistent but non-significant improvement (+1.4\\,pp, $p\\approx 0.19$). Cross-batch calibration or joint decoding for NER, and broader validation of RE batch composition, are the key next steps.\n",
      "numLines": 17,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 17
    }
  },

I found comprehensive information about the ACL Rolling Review (ARR) 2025 reviewer guidelines. Here's a summary of the key information:

## Three Scoring Criteria

Starting with February 2025, ARR reviewers are asked to provide three scores: soundness, excitement, and overall recommendation.

### Soundness Score

The soundness scores must be justified by the text of the review. If you give a low Soundness score without finding any major faults, this means that your review is not a faithful explanation of your recommendation, and you need to revise it.

### Excitement Score

Sometimes you may find the work sound, but not something that is interesting for you personally. Such preferences are more subjective and should be reflected in a separate excitement score, which is orthogonal to the assessment of the soundness of the paper. A score of 5 = Highly Exciting means: "I would recommend this paper to others and/or attend its presentation in a conference."

### Overall Recommendation

The main criteria for Findings are soundness and reproducibility. Conference recommendations may also consider novelty, impact and other factors.

## Additional Guidelines

Starting in May 2025, ARR also adopts the new policy on the 'highly irresponsible' (meta-)reviewers. The program chairs and ARR EiCs will consider the egregious cases reported by chairs (on their own judgement or based on author reports), including the cases of extremely terse reviews, unprofessional tone, and LLM-generated reviews.

For detailed guidance, you can access the full reviewer guidelines at http://aclrollingreview.org/reviewerguidelines and the review form at http://aclrollingreview.org/reviewform.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PWrZSYwgE3ig2iN7qEfF94`</summary>

```text
# ACL ARR Review Form: Scoring Dimensions

## 1. Reviewer Confidence
Scale: 1-5
- **5**: "Positive that my evaluation is correct. I read the paper very carefully..."
- **4**: Quite sure; unlikely to have missed important points
- **3**: Reasonably confident, though some details may have been overlooked
- **2**: Willing to defend evaluation, but likely missed some details
- **1**: Outside expertise area or paper is very difficult to understand

## 2. Soundness
Scale: 1-5 (with .5 increments)
- **5**: "Excellent: This study is one of the most thorough I have seen..."
- **4**: Strong study with sufficient support; minor experiments optional
- **3**: Acceptable; main claims supported, minor details need work
- **2**: Poor; main claims lack support; major technical problems
- **1**: "Major Issues: This study is not yet sufficiently thorough..."

## 3. Excitement
Scale: 1-5 (with .5 increments)
- **5**: Highly exciting; would recommend to others
- **4**: Exciting; would mention to colleagues
- **3**: Interesting; might mention selectively
- **2**: Potentially interesting; doesn't resonate personally
- **1**: Not exciting; unlikely to resonate with broader community

## 4. Overall Assessment
Scale: 1-5 (with .5 increments)
- **5**: Consider for Award (top 2.5% papers)
- **4**: Conference acceptance
- **3**: Findings acceptance
- **2**: Resubmit after revisions
- **1**: Do not resubmit

## 5. Reproducibility
Scale: 1-5
- **5**: Results easily reproducible
- **4**: Mostly reproducible; minor variations possible
- **3**: Reproducible with difficulty; underspecified parameters
- **2**: Hard to reproduce; unavailable data or insufficient details
- **1**: Cannot reproduce results

## 6. Datasets (if applicable)
Scale: 1-5
- **5**: Enabling for future research decisions
- **4**: Useful for other researchers
- **3**: Potentially useful
- **2**: Documentary value only
- **1**: No usable datasets

## 7. Software (if applicable)
Scale: 1-5
- **5**: Enabling for future research
- **4**: Useful for practitioners
- **3**: Potentially useful
- **2**: Documentary value
- **1**: No usable software

## 8. Author Identity Knowledge
Scale: 1-5
- **5**: Know/can guess author from double-blind violation
- **4**: Know from preprints or workshops
- **3**: Deduced from submission content
- **2**: Know from informal sources
- **1**: No educated guess

## Key Evaluation Guidelines

Reviewers should assess:
- **Correctness** of results and argumentation
- **Clarity** of exposition and organization
- **Novelty** and usefulness of methodology
- **Impact** in broad or narrow subfields
- **Limitations** and societal implications
- **Ethical considerations** per ACL code
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Now I have all the information I need. Let me compile my review. Let me carefully analyze the paper across all the dimensions I've been asked to focus on:

1. **ACL format compliance**
2. **Writing quality**
3. **Figure/Table quality**
4. **Reproducibility**
5. **Checklist review**
6. **p-value and CI format consistency**

Let me go through each systematically.

### 1. ACL Format Compliance

- **Page limit**: The paper uses `\usepackage[review]{acl}` which is correct. Let me estimate page count. The main body includes: abstract, introduction, related_work, experimental_setup, results, sdgd_negative, discussion (which includes conclusion). 

Estimating from the tex source is tricky, but I can see the paper has substantial content with multiple tables and figures spread across sections like Introduction, Related Work, Experimental Setup, Results, and Discussion. I'll note this as something to monitor but won't make a definitive claim without seeing the compiled PDF.

- **Limitations section**: I can see it's included in the main document.

The structure looks correct per ACL guidelines—Limitations comes after the main body but before references, with the checklist also positioned before the bibliography. The author is anonymized as "Anonymous," so I need to scan through for any self-citations that might compromise anonymity.

Now I'm checking the references format and noticing some entries cite papers from 2025, which raises a timing question since the submission is supposedly for ACL ARR in May 2025, but the current date appears to be 2026. I'm noticing several papers cited with publication dates after the May 2025 deadline—EMNLP 2025, ACL 2025 findings, and system demos that haven't occurred yet. Most problematically, there's an arXiv paper from August 2025 being cited in a May 2025 submission, which is temporally impossible. The ACM Web Conference 2025 papers might barely work depending on timing, but this inconsistency suggests either the submission is retrospective or there's a fundamental dating error in the bibliography. I'm spotting temporal inconsistencies across several citations — papers with publication dates after the May 2025 submission deadline, including arXiv preprints from July and August 2025. The Responsible NLP Research Checklist looks thorough, so that's good. Now I'm moving on to assess the writing quality, starting with the abstract, which is quite dense and runs long as a single paragraph.

Looking at the section structure, there's a notable imbalance — the Introduction is substantial with a clear contributions list, Related Work is brief, Experimental Setup is thorough, but Results dominates at around 154 lines. Meanwhile, Section 5 addressing RQ3 is surprisingly short for a core research question, and the Discussion and Conclusion combined are thin. I'm also checking terminology consistency across the paper.

The acronyms "Schema Confusability Score" and "Batch Confusability Score" are used consistently, though "Channel 1" and "Channel 2" feel informal. There's a disconnect where the paper references "Schema-DAG-Guided Decomposition (SDGD)" but the section title doesn't fully explain what the DAG component means or why it matters in the main text. The number density is particularly high in Results, which hurts readability overall.

Now examining the figures in the main text, starting with Figure 1's dual-panel layout showing count curves and BCS scaling behavior. Looking at the appendix tables, some of the core findings—particularly the error taxonomy breakdown and SDGD results—might actually deserve space in the main text rather than being relegated to the appendix. I'm also noticing the figure paths use relative references that could break if the build directory isn't configured correctly, and there's an unused SDGD comparison figure that could potentially fill a gap in that section. On the reproducibility front, the vLLM version listed (v0.20.2) seems outdated compared to current 2025 versions, which raises questions about whether the experimental setup details are current.

The DocRED sampling procedure is particularly vague—they mention "balanced instances" but don't specify how many, which is a significant reproducibility gap compared to the explicit sample sizes given for FewRel and Few-NERD. Combined with the lack of an anonymous code repository link and the promise to release code only upon acceptance, there are several barriers to reproducing this work. I'm noticing the p-value formatting is all over the place throughout the paper — some use scientific notation like `$p < 10^{-6}$`, others write it out as `$p < 0.001$`, and a few give exact values with high precision like `$p = 2.56 \times 10^{-26}$`. I should standardize this across the manuscript for consistency.

The confidence interval formatting is more uniform with square brackets, though whether the "95%" prefix appears varies inconsistently.

Now I'm compiling the key issues I've identified for the review: the temporal impossibility in the references, the unspecified DocRED sample size that affects reproducibility, the questionable vLLM version claim, and the fact that section 5 on SDGD is too underdeveloped.

I'm also noting several warnings—only two figures in the main text, an abstract packed with too many numbers, and a thin discussion section. There are minor issues with p-value formatting inconsistency, vague AI assistant disclosure, and an unused figure file.

I've verified that while a deprecated conclusion.tex file exists, it's not actually included in the document; the discussion.tex properly contains both Discussion and Conclusion sections, which is structurally fine. I'm checking whether the Limitations section should be numbered or unnumbered according to ACL guidelines — it should be unnumbered, which the paper correctly implements with `\section*{Limitations}`. Now I'm verifying the proper ordering of sections in ACL format: the main body comes first, followed by Limitations, then Ethics Statement if needed, References, and Appendix, with the checklist placement varying depending on the specific template requirements.

The paper places the checklist between Limitations and Bibliography, which is slightly non-standard but shouldn't cause issues since checklist sections don't count toward page limits. I also notice the paper uses `\usepackage[normalem]{ulem}` for strikethrough formatting in the prompt appendix and `\usepackage{listings}` for code blocks, both reasonable choices. However, I'm noticing that while the paper extensively discusses DocRED experiments, the DocRED prompt template is missing from the appendix — only the NER template appears to be included. Few-NERD), ED (MAVEN), and RE (FewRel) prompts, but not the DocRED prompt specifically. Since DocRED is described as using the same formulation as FewRel (§3.1), this might be adequate, but it should be explicit.

Let me now compile all of this into my review format.

Actually, one more thing to check — I should verify whether the paper's section numbering would work. The sections are:
1. Introduction
2. Related Work
3. Experimental Setup
4. Results
5. Schema Decomposition (SDGD)
6. Discussion
7. Conclusion

Wait, Discussion and Conclusion are in the same file. Let me re-read:
```
\section{Discussion}
\label{sec:discussion}
...
\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}
```

So they are separate numbered sections. With the numbering: §1 Introduction, §2 Related Work, §3 Experimental Setup, §4 Results, §5 Schema Decomposition, §6 Discussion, §7 Conclusion. That's 7 sections total, which is reasonable, though Discussion is quite brief at 3 paragraphs and Conclusion is just one paragraph — they could potentially be merged. The deprecated conclusion.tex file doesn't cause issues since main.tex pulls from discussion.tex instead, which contains both sections. Looking at the approximate page breakdown, I'm seeing roughly 0.3 pages for the abstract, 1.5 for introduction with contributions, 0.5 for related work, and about 2 pages for experimental setup with tables and equations.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the information I need. Let me compile my comprehensive review. Let me carefully go through each dimension.

Let me also double-check a few things:

1. The abstract word count - let me estimate more carefully from the abstract.tex content.

2. The DocRED sample size issue - §3.1 says "We sample balanced instances" without a specific number. FewRel says 1,000, Few-NERD says 1,000. Table 1 says DocRED has 1,000 eval units. So actually the number IS in the table. But the text doesn't explicitly state it. 

3. For the figures, I'm checking what's actually used in the paper. The directory contains four PDF files, but only three appear in the manuscript - the count curves and scaling plots are combined in Figure 1, the error taxonomy is Figure 2, and the comparison plot isn't referenced anywhere.

4. Now I'm looking more carefully at the temporal inconsistencies in the references. The submission deadline is May 25, 2025, but several citations reference papers that wouldn't have existed by then - the reflect-learn paper from AAAI 2026 with an August 2025 arXiv date, and the GLiNER 2 system demo paper from July 2025 are both temporally impossible.

For the others like the ACL 2025 Findings and EMNLP 2025 papers, they might be acceptable if earlier arXiv versions existed before the deadline, but the bibliography entries link to ACL Anthology URLs that wouldn't be live yet. The ACM Web Conference 2025 paper could potentially work depending on its exact publication date. The most egregious violations are definitely the reflect-learn and GLiNER 2 citations.

But I'm realizing the context here matters — if this paper is being written in 2026 as a retroactive submission to a May 2025 venue, that explains the temporal misalignment. The stated deadline is May 2025, but the actual writing happened later. As a reviewer checking format compliance against that May 2025 target, I should flag these inconsistencies even if there's a reasonable explanation.

Looking at the Limitations section placement, it does appear after the Conclusion in the main.tex file, which matches what the checklist requires. The positioning before references is also correct for ACL format.

I'm noticing the checklist includes an E section on AI-Assisted Research that doesn't appear in the standard ARR checklist — this seems to be a custom addition.

For the bibliography setup, the paper uses `\bibliography{references}` without an explicit `\bibliographystyle` command, but the acl package handles this automatically so it's fine.

The paper title runs 13 words, which is lengthy but still acceptable. I think I have what I need to write the full review now.

Looking at the scoring dimensions: soundness is solid with controlled experiments and careful statistics, though the SDGD section's conclusions are limited by narrow configurations—around 3.5-4 out of 5. The work addresses a real problem and provides the first controlled study, but the contributions are incremental with modest effect sizes and limited practical impact, especially for NER—that's about 3 out of 5 for excitement. Reproducibility has gaps: DocRED sampling details are vague, the vLLM version is unclear, and no code is released, so 3 out of 5 there. Overall this is a careful analytical study with solid methodology but modest practical gains, landing around 3-3.5 out of 5 for the findings themselves.

But I should focus on what I'm actually evaluating—presentation quality, format compliance, and reproducibility. The writing is technically sound but dense, with good statistical transparency, though the structure feels unbalanced with thin sections on results and discussion. The abstract is packed too tightly, there are only two main figures, some temporal references are off, and the p-value formatting isn't consistent throughout. Presentation quality comes out to about 3 out of 5—competent work that could use clearer organization and tighter writing.

`\\author{Anonymous}`，无 self-citation 泄露，无 Acknowledgements |\n| References 格式 | ⚠️ | 整体一致，但部分引用仅有 aclanthology URL（无 DOI），少数有 DOI（`cmas-ner-2025`, `lost-in-middle-2024`）。格式不完全统一 |\n| Checklist 位置 | ⚠️ | 置于 Limitations 和 Bibliography 之间。标准 ACL 模板通常将 checklist 放在最末（appendix 之后）。不影响审稿但非标准 |\n\n---\n\n## 主要优势\n\n- **统计透明度高**：全文一致地报告 95% bootstrap CI、permutation test p-values、effect sizes ($r$, $r^2$, $R^2$)，远超多数 NLP 论文水准。\n- **实验设计严谨**：count curves 和 BCS-controlled 两种 paradigm 互补，within-pool 分析控制了 pool composition 混淆变量。\n- **诚实报告负面结果**：SDGD 失败、factorial test 不显著、pairwise effect size modest 等均如实报告，体现了良好的科学实践。\n- **Limitations section 充实**：6 条具体限制，涵盖 model coverage、effect size、metric scope 等，内容翔实。\n- **Prompt templates 完整**：Appendix 提供了完整的 prompt 模板和 v1→v2 差异标注，有利于复现。",
